**Objective**: Generate a semantically equivalent vectorize-friendly version of `*_opt` using:  
**Preservation Requirements**:  
1. Function signature

If the original value is used, e.g. a[i+1] is used for iteration i, a[i+1] should be stored in a temporary variable(use memcpy to store orign value) or used before updating a[i+1]
If the updated value is used, e.g. a[i-1] is used for iteration i, a[i-1] should update before using it.

**Possible Transformations**  
Loop Splitting:  
**Type 1 - Instruction Splitting**  
   *When to use*:  
   - Multiple independent statements exist within the loop body  
   - Example pattern:  
     ```c
     for(i) { 
       A[i] = ...;  // Independent group 1
       B[i] = ...;  // Independent group 2 
     }
     ```  
   *Implementation*:  
   ```c
   for(i) { A[i] = ...; }  // Split loop 1  
   for(i) { B[i] = ...; }  // Split loop 2  
   ```  
   *Validation*:  
   - Total iteration count remains identical  
   - Memory access order preserved across all arrays  

**Type 2 - Iteration Range Splitting**  
   *When to use*:  
   - Loop contains phase-dependent patterns (e.g., mid-point update)  
   - Vectorization blocked by conditional value changes  
   - Example pattern:  
     ```c
     for(i=0; i<LEN; i++) a[i] = a[mid] + b[i];  
     ```  
   *Implementation*:  
   ```c
   // Phase 1: i=0~mid (pre-update)  
   for(i=0; i<mid; i++) a[i] = a[mid] + b[i];  
   // Mid-point update  
   a[mid] = a[mid] + b[mid];  
   // Phase 2: i=mid+1~end (post-update)  
   for(i=mid+1; i<LEN; i++) a[i] = a[mid] + b[i];  
   ```  
   *Validation Checklist*:  
   - Split boundaries cover original iteration range  
   - No overlapping writes between split segments  

Loop Reordering and Instruction Reordering:
**Application Rules**:  
   *Priority Patterns*:  
   - Move loop-invariant computations upward  
     ```c
     // Before reorder:
     a[i] = x + y;  // y is invariant
     b[i] = y * z;
     
     // After reorder:
     b[i] = y * z;  // y used earlier
     a[i] = x + y;
     ```  
   - Solve loop dependency
    ```c
    // Before reorder:
     a[i] = b[i-1] + y;  // y is invariant
     b[i] = y * z;
     
     // After reorder:
     b[i] = y * z;  // y used earlier
     a[i] = b[i-1] + y;
     // Before reorder:
     a[i] = b[i-1] + y;  // y is invariant
     b[i] = a[i+1] * z;
     
     // After reorder:
     b[i] = a[i+1] * z;
     a[i] = b[i-1] + y;
     ```
   - Cluster memory operations with similar addresses 

Loop Distribution:
**When to Apply**:  
   - Multiple independent write targets in loop body  
   - Example:  
     ```c
     // Original
     for(i) {
       A[i] = ...;  // Writes to A
       B[i] = ...;  // Writes to B (independent)
     }
     
     // Distributed
     for(i) { A[i] = ...; }  
     for(i) { B[i] = ...; }  
     ```  
   *Validation*:  
   - Distributed loops must have identical iteration ranges  
   - No interleaved access between distributed loops  

Use temporary variable:
Use temp variable to store result to avoid complex memory access

Remove branching logic (if, else if, switch) from loops to enable automatic vectorization:
Replace multi-way conditionals with arithmetic masking, ensuring uniform execution paths across iterations.
**When to Apply**:  
    - Example:  
     ```c
    // Original
    if (cond1)  
        x = value_A;  
    else if (cond2)  
        x = value_B;  
    else  
        x = value_C;
    // Optimized  
    x = cond1 * value_A + (cond2 && !cond1) * value_B + (!cond1 && !cond2) * value_C;  
     ```

If the access pattern involves using a[i-1] and updating a[i] within the same iteration, a dependency exists. Attempt to reorder the instructions to resolve this dependency.

void s211_opt(int iterations, int LEN_1D, float*a, float*b,float*c,float*d,float*e)

            // Prepare b_next for next iteration

}

## FEEDBACK OUTPUT
Let’s go step-by-step.

---

### **1. Compilation errors in optimized code**
The optimized code compiles without syntax errors.  
The compiler feedback is a remark about vectorization failure, not a compilation error, so we proceed.

---

### **2. Semantic comparison with simple test cases**

**Original loop (for nl = 0, i = 1 to LEN_1D-2):**  

For each `i` in original:
```
a[i] = b[i-1] + c[i] * d[i];
b[i] = b[i+1] - e[i] * d[i];
```
Important: `b[i]` is updated in the same iteration, but `b[i-1]` used in `a[i]` is from **before** this iteration’s update (since loop goes forward i=1,2,...).

---

**Optimized code logic:**
```
b_next = b[1];
for i = 1 to LEN_1D-2:
    a[i] = b[i-1] + c[i] * d[i];
    temp = b[i+1];
    b[i] = temp - e[i] * d[i];
    b_next = temp;
```
Here `b[i-1]` is the value from **before** this iteration’s update? Let’s check carefully:

When `i=1`:
- `b[i-1] = b[0]` → original `b[0]` (unchanged so far, correct).
- `temp = b[2]` (original `b[2]`).
- `b[1] = b[2] - e[1]*d[1]` (correct original formula).

When `i=2`:
- `b[i-1] = b[1]` → but `b[1]` was overwritten in previous iteration!  
  In original code, `a[2] = b[1]` uses **original** `b[1]` (from before any updates in this nl iteration).  
  In optimized code, `b[1]` is already updated to `b[2] - e[1]*d[1]`, which is **wrong** for `a[2]`.

Thus, the optimized code has a **semantic error**: `a[i]` for `i>1` uses the updated `b[i-1]` from current nl iteration, not the original `b[i-1]`.

---

**Example verification** (LEN_1D=5, iterations=1, all c,d,e=0 for simplicity, b = [0,1,2,3,4]):

Original:
i=1: a[1]=b[0]=0, b[1]=b[2]=2  
i=2: a[2]=b[1] (original b[1]=1) =1, b[2]=b[3]=3  
i=3: a[3]=b[2] (original b[2]=2) =2, b[3]=b[4]=4  
Results: a=[?,0,1,2,?], b=[0,2,3,4,4]

Optimized:
i=1: a[1]=b[0]=0, temp=b[2]=2, b[1]=2, b_next=2  
i=2: a[2]=b[1]=2 (wrong! should be 1), temp=b[3]=3, b[2]=3, b_next=3  
i=3: a[3]=b[2]=3 (wrong! should be 2), temp=b[4]=4, b[3]=4, b_next=4  
Results: a=[?,0,2,3,?], b=[0,2,3,4,4] → a values differ.

---

**Conclusion:** The semantics are **incorrect**.  
The optimization incorrectly reuses `b[i-1]` after it has been overwritten.

---

### **3. Stop and output**
Since semantics are wrong, we stop here.

---

   // Mid-point update  
   a[mid] = a[mid] + b[mid];  
   // Phase 2: i=mid+1~end (post-update)  
   for(i=mid+1; i<LEN; i++) a[i] = a[mid] + b[i];  
   ```

   - Split boundaries cover original iteration range  
   - No overlapping writes between split segments  

Loop Reordering and Instruction Reordering:
**Application Rules**:  
   *Priority Patterns*:  
   - Move loop-invariant computations upward  
     ```c
     // Before reorder:
     a[i] = x + y;  // y is invariant
     b[i] = y * z;
     
     // After reorder:
     b[i] = y * z;  // y used earlier
     a[i] = x + y;
     ```  
   - Solve loop dependency
    ```c
    // Before reorder:
     a[i] = b[i-1] + y;  // y is invariant
     b[i] = y * z;
     
     // After reorder:
     b[i] = y * z;  // y used earlier
     a[i] = b[i-1] + y;
     // Before reorder:
     a[i] = b[i-1] + y;  // y is invariant
     b[i] = a[i+1] * z;
     
     // After reorder:
     b[i] = a[i+1] * z;
     a[i] = b[i-1] + y;
     ```
   - Cluster memory operations with similar addresses 

Loop Distribution:
**When to Apply**:  
   - Multiple independent write targets in loop body  
   - Example:  
     ```c
     // Original
     for(i) {
       A[i] = ...;  // Writes to A
       B[i] = ...;  // Writes to B (independent)
     }
     
     // Distributed
     for(i) { A[i] = ...; }  
     for(i) { B[i] = ...; }  
     ```  
   *Validation*:  
   - Distributed loops must have identical iteration ranges  
   - No interleaved access between distributed loops  

Use temporary variable:
Use temp variable to store result to avoid complex memory access

Remove branching logic (if, else if, switch) from loops to enable automatic vectorization:
Replace multi-way conditionals with arithmetic masking, ensuring uniform execution paths across iterations.
**When to Apply**:  
    - Example:  
     ```c
    // Original
    if (cond1)  
        x = value_A;  
    else if (cond2)  
        x = value_B;  
    else  
        x = value_C;
    // Optimized  
    x = cond1 * value_A + (cond2 && !cond1) * value_B + (!cond1 && !cond2) * value_C;  
     ```

for (int i = 1; i < LEN_1D-1; i++) {

In the original, `b[i]` is updated in the same iteration after being used for `a[i]`.  
The next iteration uses `b[i]` (now updated) as `b[i-1]` for computing `a[i+1]`? Wait — check carefully:  

- For `i = 1`:  
  `a[1] = b[0] + c[1]*d[1]`

- For `i = 2`:  
  `a[2] = b[1] + c[2]*d[2]`  
  Here `b[1]` is the **new** value from iteration `i=1`.  

So there is a **true dependency** from `b[i]` (write in iteration `i`) to `a[i+1]` (read `b[i]` in next iteration).

**Optimized code** splits into two separate loops:  

```c
for (int i = 1; i < LEN_1D-1; i++) {

}

Now in the first loop, all `a[i]` use the **original** `b[i-1]` from before the loop starts (or from previous `nl` iteration’s final `b`).  
In the second loop, `b[i]` is updated using `b[i+1]` (original `b[i+1]` from before this loop).  

But in the original, `b[i]` update used `b[i+1]` **before** `b[i+1]` was updated in that same iteration? Let’s check:

So `b[2]` is read in iteration `i=1` before being overwritten in iteration `i=2`. That’s fine — no loop-carried dependency on `b[i+1]` for the `b[i]` update.  

But the **key** is:  
In original, `a[2]` uses `b[1]` **after** `b[1]` was updated in iteration `i=1`.  
In optimized, `a[2]` uses `b[1]` **before** `b[1]` is updated (because all `a[i]` computed first).  

Thus semantics differ.

   // Phase 2: i=mid+1~end (post-update)

**Objective**: Generate a semantically equivalent vectorize-friendly version of `*_opt` using:  
**Preservation Requirements**:  
1. Function signature

If the original value is used, e.g. a[i+1] is used for iteration i, a[i+1] should be stored in a temporary variable(use memcpy to store orign value) or used before updating a[i+1]
If the updated value is used, e.g. a[i-1] is used for iteration i, a[i-1] should update before using it.

**Possible Transformations**  
Loop Splitting:  
**Type 1 - Instruction Splitting**  
   *When to use*:  
   - Multiple independent statements exist within the loop body  
   - Example pattern:  
     ```c
     for(i) { 
       A[i] = ...;  // Independent group 1
       B[i] = ...;  // Independent group 2 
     }
     ```  
   *Implementation*:  
   ```c
   for(i) { A[i] = ...; }  // Split loop 1  
   for(i) { B[i] = ...; }  // Split loop 2  
   ```  
   *Validation*:  
   - Total iteration count remains identical  
   - Memory access order preserved across all arrays  

**Type 2 - Iteration Range Splitting**  
   *When to use*:  
   - Loop contains phase-dependent patterns (e.g., mid-point update)  
   - Vectorization blocked by conditional value changes  
   - Example pattern:  
     ```c
     for(i=0; i<LEN; i++) a[i] = a[mid] + b[i];  
     ```  
   *Implementation*:  
   ```c
   // Phase 1: i=0~mid (pre-update)  
   for(i=0; i<mid; i++) a[i] = a[mid] + b[i];  
   // Mid-point update  
   a[mid] = a[mid] + b[mid];  
   // Phase 2: i=mid+1~end (post-update)  
   for(i=mid+1; i<LEN; i++) a[i] = a[mid] + b[i];  
   ```  
   *Validation Checklist*:  
   - Split boundaries cover original iteration range  
   - No overlapping writes between split segments  

Loop Reordering and Instruction Reordering:
**Application Rules**:  
   *Priority Patterns*:  
   - Move loop-invariant computations upward  
     ```c
     // Before reorder:
     a[i] = x + y;  // y is invariant
     b[i] = y * z;
     
     // After reorder:
     b[i] = y * z;  // y used earlier
     a[i] = x + y;
     ```  
   - Solve loop dependency
    ```c
    // Before reorder:
     a[i] = b[i-1] + y;  // y is invariant
     b[i] = y * z;
     
     // After reorder:
     b[i] = y * z;  // y used earlier
     a[i] = b[i-1] + y;
     // Before reorder:
     a[i] = b[i-1] + y;  // y is invariant
     b[i] = a[i+1] * z;
     
     // After reorder:
     b[i] = a[i+1] * z;
     a[i] = b[i-1] + y;
     ```
   - Cluster memory operations with similar addresses 

Loop Distribution:
**When to Apply**:  
   - Multiple independent write targets in loop body  
   - Example:  
     ```c
     // Original
     for(i) {
       A[i] = ...;  // Writes to A
       B[i] = ...;  // Writes to B (independent)
     }
     
     // Distributed
     for(i) { A[i] = ...; }  
     for(i) { B[i] = ...; }  
     ```  
   *Validation*:  
   - Distributed loops must have identical iteration ranges  
   - No interleaved access between distributed loops  

Use temporary variable:
Use temp variable to store result to avoid complex memory access

Remove branching logic (if, else if, switch) from loops to enable automatic vectorization:
Replace multi-way conditionals with arithmetic masking, ensuring uniform execution paths across iterations.
**When to Apply**:  
    - Example:  
     ```c
    // Original
    if (cond1)  
        x = value_A;  
    else if (cond2)  
        x = value_B;  
    else  
        x = value_C;
    // Optimized  
    x = cond1 * value_A + (cond2 && !cond1) * value_B + (!cond1 && !cond2) * value_C;  
     ```

**Possible Transformations**  
Loop Splitting:  
**Type 1 - Instruction Splitting**  
   *When to use*:  
   - Multiple independent statements exist within the loop body  
   - Example pattern:  
     ```c
     for(i) { 
       A[i] = ...;  // Independent group 1
       B[i] = ...;  // Independent group 2 
     }
     ```  
   *Implementation*:  
   ```c
   for(i) { A[i] = ...; }  // Split loop 1  
   for(i) { B[i] = ...; }  // Split loop 2  
   ```  
   *Validation*:  
   - Total iteration count remains identical  
   - Memory access order preserved across all arrays  

**Type 2 - Iteration Range Splitting**  
   *When to use*:  
   - Loop contains phase-dependent patterns (e.g., mid-point update)  
   - Vectorization blocked by conditional value changes  
   - Example pattern:  
     ```c
     for(i=0; i<LEN; i++) a[i] = a[mid] + b[i];  
     ```  
   *Implementation*:  
   ```c
   // Phase 1: i=0~mid (pre-update)  
   for(i=0; i<mid; i++) a[i] = a[mid] + b[i];  
   // Mid-point update  
   a[mid] = a[mid] + b[mid];  
   // Phase 2: i=mid+1~end (post-update)  
   for(i=mid+1; i<LEN; i++) a[i] = a[mid] + b[i];  
   ```  
   *Validation Checklist*:  
   - Split boundaries cover original iteration range  
   - No overlapping writes between split segments  

Loop Reordering and Instruction Reordering:
**Application Rules**:  
   *Priority Patterns*:  
   - Move loop-invariant computations upward  
     ```c
     // Before reorder:
     a[i] = x + y;  // y is invariant
     b[i] = y * z;
     
     // After reorder:
     b[i] = y * z;  // y used earlier
     a[i] = x + y;
     ```  
   - Solve loop dependency
    ```c
    // Before reorder:
     a[i] = b[i-1] + y;  // y is invariant
     b[i] = y * z;
     
     // After reorder:
     b[i] = y * z;  // y used earlier
     a[i] = b[i-1] + y;
     // Before reorder:
     a[i] = b[i-1] + y;  // y is invariant
     b[i] = a[i+1] * z;
     
     // After reorder:
     b[i] = a[i+1] * z;
     a[i] = b[i-1] + y;
     ```
   - Cluster memory operations with similar addresses 

Loop Distribution:
**When to Apply**:  
   - Multiple independent write targets in loop body  
   - Example:  
     ```c
     // Original
     for(i) {
       A[i] = ...;  // Writes to A
       B[i] = ...;  // Writes to B (independent)
     }
     
     // Distributed
     for(i) { A[i] = ...; }  
     for(i) { B[i] = ...; }  
     ```  
   *Validation*:  
   - Distributed loops must have identical iteration ranges  
   - No interleaved access between distributed loops  

Use temporary variable:
Use temp variable to store result to avoid complex memory access

Replace multi-way conditionals with arithmetic masking, ensuring uniform execution paths across iterations.

        x = value_A;

        x = value_B;  
    else  
        x = value_C;
    // Optimized  
    x = cond1 * value_A + (cond2 && !cond1) * value_B + (!cond1 && !cond2) * value_C;

Compiler feedback:

This indicates a **loop-carried dependency** on `b_next` (updated `b[i]`) that is used in the next iteration’s `a[i+1]` calculation.  
This is a **true dependency** (RAW) across iterations, which prevents vectorization in the current form.  

**Why vectorization fails:**  
- The computation of `a[i]` depends on `b[i-1]` from the previous iteration (updated value).  
- This is a serial recurrence, not a reduction or easily parallelizable pattern.  

**Optimization opportunity:**  
The original loop can be **split** into two separate loops:  
1. Compute all `a[i]` using original `b[i-1]` (but this requires original `b[i-1]`, which is overwritten).  
2. Compute all `b[i]` using original `b[i+1]`.  

However, because `b[i]` is overwritten in the same iteration, and `a[i+1]` needs the updated `b[i]`, the two statements are inherently sequential across iterations.  

But note:  
`a[i] = b[i-1] + c[i]*d[i]`  
`b[i] = b[i+1] - e[i]*d[i]`  

The `b[i-1]` used in `a[i]` is **updated** from iteration `i-1`.  
The `b[i+1]` used in `b[i]` is **original** from the array.  

This means the loop over `i` for `b[i]` updates can be done **independently** if we store original `b[i+1]` values before overwriting.  
But `a[i]` depends on updated `b[i-1]`, which is a recurrence:  
`b[i] = original_b[i+1] - e[i]*d[i]`  
`a[i+1] = b[i] + c[i+1]*d[i+1]`  

Thus, `a[i+1]` depends on `b[i]`, which depends on `original_b[i+1]`.  
So `a[i+1]` can be computed once `original_b[i+1]` is known, but `b[i]` is computed in the same iteration.  

Actually, we can rewrite:  
`a[i] = b[i-1] + c[i]*d[i]`  
`b[i-1]` is from previous iteration’s update: `b[i-1] = original_b[i] - e[i-1]*d[i-1]`.  

So:  
`a[i] = (original_b[i] - e[i-1]*d[i-1]) + c[i]*d[i]`  

This shows `a[i]` depends on `original_b[i]` and `e[i-1]`, `d[i-1]`, `c[i]`, `d[i]`.  
Thus, `a[i]` can be computed in parallel if we have `original_b[i]` saved.  
Similarly, `b[i] = original_b[i+1] - e[i]*d[i]` is parallelizable.  

Therefore, the loop can be vectorized by:  
1. Saving `original_b[i]` for all i.  
2. Vectorized computation of `b[i] = original_b[i+1] - e[i]*d[i]`.  
3. Vectorized computation of `a[i] = original_b[i] - e[i-1]*d[i-1] + c[i]*d[i]` (with shift for i=1..LEN_1D-1).  

But the current optimized code does **not** do this; it still has a serial recurrence via `b_next`.  

**Conclusion:**  
The optimized code matches semantics but is **not vectorized** due to the carried dependency. There is an optimization opportunity to enable vectorization by splitting the recurrence.  

Since the compiler says “not vectorized” and there is an opportunity to improve, we must output **FAIL**.

**Preservation Requirements**:  
1. Function signature

If the original value is used, e.g. a[i+1] is used for iteration i, a[i+1] should be stored in a temporary variable(use memcpy to store orign value) or used before updating a[i+1]

**Possible Transformations**  
Loop Splitting:  
**Type 1 - Instruction Splitting**  
   *When to use*:  
   - Multiple independent statements exist within the loop body  
   - Example pattern:  
     ```c
     for(i) { 
       A[i] = ...;  // Independent group 1
       B[i] = ...;  // Independent group 2 
     }
     ```  
   *Implementation*:  
   ```c
   for(i) { A[i] = ...; }  // Split loop 1  
   for(i) { B[i] = ...; }  // Split loop 2  
   ```  
   *Validation*:  
   - Total iteration count remains identical  
   - Memory access order preserved across all arrays  

**Type 2 - Iteration Range Splitting**  
   *When to use*:  
   - Loop contains phase-dependent patterns (e.g., mid-point update)  
   - Vectorization blocked by conditional value changes  
   - Example pattern:  
     ```c
     for(i=0; i<LEN; i++) a[i] = a[mid] + b[i];  
     ```  
   *Implementation*:  
   ```c
   // Phase 1: i=0~mid (pre-update)  
   for(i=0; i<mid; i++) a[i] = a[mid] + b[i];  
   // Mid-point update  
   a[mid] = a[mid] + b[mid];  
   // Phase 2: i=mid+1~end (post-update)  
   for(i=mid+1; i<LEN; i++) a[i] = a[mid] + b[i];  
   ```  
   *Validation Checklist*:  
   - Split boundaries cover original iteration range  
   - No overlapping writes between split segments  

Loop Reordering and Instruction Reordering:
**Application Rules**:  
   *Priority Patterns*:  
   - Move loop-invariant computations upward  
     ```c
     // Before reorder:
     a[i] = x + y;  // y is invariant
     b[i] = y * z;
     
     // After reorder:
     b[i] = y * z;  // y used earlier
     a[i] = x + y;
     ```  
   - Solve loop dependency
    ```c
    // Before reorder:
     a[i] = b[i-1] + y;  // y is invariant
     b[i] = y * z;
     
     // After reorder:
     b[i] = y * z;  // y used earlier
     a[i] = b[i-1] + y;
     // Before reorder:
     a[i] = b[i-1] + y;  // y is invariant
     b[i] = a[i+1] * z;
     
     // After reorder:
     b[i] = a[i+1] * z;
     a[i] = b[i-1] + y;
     ```
   - Cluster memory operations with similar addresses 

Loop Distribution:
**When to Apply**:  
   - Multiple independent write targets in loop body  
   - Example:  
     ```c
     // Original
     for(i) {
       A[i] = ...;  // Writes to A
       B[i] = ...;  // Writes to B (independent)
     }
     
     // Distributed
     for(i) { A[i] = ...; }  
     for(i) { B[i] = ...; }  
     ```  
   *Validation*:  
   - Distributed loops must have identical iteration ranges  
   - No interleaved access between distributed loops  

Use temporary variable:
Use temp variable to store result to avoid complex memory access

Remove branching logic (if, else if, switch) from loops to enable automatic vectorization:
Replace multi-way conditionals with arithmetic masking, ensuring uniform execution paths across iterations.
**When to Apply**:  
    - Example:  
     ```c
    // Original
    if (cond1)  
        x = value_A;  
    else if (cond2)  
        x = value_B;  
    else  
        x = value_C;
    // Optimized  
    x = cond1 * value_A + (cond2 && !cond1) * value_B + (!cond1 && !cond2) * value_C;  
     ```

**1. Compilation Errors in Optimized Code:**
The optimized code contains a critical compilation error. The use of `__builtin_alloca()` inside the loop is problematic because:
- `alloca` allocates memory on the stack, but allocating `LEN_1D * sizeof(float)` bytes repeatedly in each iteration could cause stack overflow
- The allocation size depends on runtime parameter `LEN_1D`, which could be large
- While not a syntax error, this is a serious runtime hazard that would likely crash the program

**2. Semantic Comparison:**
Let's trace through a simple example with LEN_1D=5:

**Original code execution (first iteration, nl=0):**
- i=1: a[1] = b[0] + c[1]*d[1]; b[1] = b[2] - e[1]*d[1]
- i=2: a[2] = b[1] + c[2]*d[2]; b[2] = b[3] - e[2]*d[2]
- i=3: a[3] = b[2] + c[3]*d[3]; b[3] = b[4] - e[3]*d[3]

- First loop: b[1] = b_orig[2] - e[1]*d[1]; b[2] = b_orig[3] - e[2]*d[2]; b[3] = b_orig[4] - e[3]*d[3]
- Second loop: a[1] = b[0] + c[1]*d[1]; a[2] = b[1] + c[2]*d[2]; a[3] = b[2] + c[3]*d[3]

The problem is in the computation of `a[i]`. In the original code, when computing `a[2]`, it uses the **newly updated** `b[1]` from the same iteration. In the optimized code, `a[2]` uses `b[1]` which was computed in the first loop, but this `b[1]` was computed using `b_orig[2]` (the original `b[2]`), not the updated `b[2]` from the same iteration.

However, looking more carefully: In the original code, `b[1] = b[2] - e[1]*d[1]` uses `b[2]` which is the **original** `b[2]` value (not yet updated in this iteration). So actually, the optimized code correctly uses `b_orig[2]` for computing `b[1]`. The dependency is:
- `b[i]` depends on original `b[i+1]` (anti-dependency)
- `a[i]` depends on updated `b[i-1]` (flow dependency)

The optimized code correctly breaks the anti-dependency by using `b_orig`. The semantics appear to be preserved.

**3. Vectorization Analysis:**
The compiler feedback shows both loops are vectorized. However, the optimized code has significant issues:
- Memory inefficiency: Allocating and copying the entire `b` array in each iteration
- Stack overflow risk with large `LEN_1D`
- Increased memory bandwidth usage

**4. Further Optimization Opportunities:**
The current implementation can be improved:
- Remove the expensive `alloca` and array copy
- Use loop distribution without copying the entire array
- Better approach: Process the loop in reverse order for `b[i]` updates to naturally break the anti-dependency

**Conclusion:**
While the semantics are preserved and the loops are vectorized according to compiler feedback, the optimized code has a serious runtime issue (potential stack overflow) and is inefficient due to unnecessary memory copying. This represents an optimization opportunity that needs to be addressed.

**Possible Transformations**  
Loop Splitting:  
**Type 1 - Instruction Splitting**  
   *When to use*:  
   - Multiple independent statements exist within the loop body  
   - Example pattern:  
     ```c
     for(i) { 
       A[i] = ...;  // Independent group 1
       B[i] = ...;  // Independent group 2 
     }
     ```  
   *Implementation*:  
   ```c
   for(i) { A[i] = ...; }  // Split loop 1  
   for(i) { B[i] = ...; }  // Split loop 2  
   ```  
   *Validation*:  
   - Total iteration count remains identical  
   - Memory access order preserved across all arrays  

**Type 2 - Iteration Range Splitting**  
   *When to use*:  
   - Loop contains phase-dependent patterns (e.g., mid-point update)  
   - Vectorization blocked by conditional value changes  
   - Example pattern:  
     ```c
     for(i=0; i<LEN; i++) a[i] = a[mid] + b[i];  
     ```  
   *Implementation*:  
   ```c
   // Phase 1: i=0~mid (pre-update)  
   for(i=0; i<mid; i++) a[i] = a[mid] + b[i];  
   // Mid-point update  
   a[mid] = a[mid] + b[mid];  
   // Phase 2: i=mid+1~end (post-update)  
   for(i=mid+1; i<LEN; i++) a[i] = a[mid] + b[i];  
   ```  
   *Validation Checklist*:  
   - Split boundaries cover original iteration range  
   - No overlapping writes between split segments  

Loop Reordering and Instruction Reordering:
**Application Rules**:  
   *Priority Patterns*:  
   - Move loop-invariant computations upward  
     ```c
     // Before reorder:
     a[i] = x + y;  // y is invariant
     b[i] = y * z;
     
     // After reorder:
     b[i] = y * z;  // y used earlier
     a[i] = x + y;
     ```  
   - Solve loop dependency
    ```c
    // Before reorder:
     a[i] = b[i-1] + y;  // y is invariant
     b[i] = y * z;
     
     // After reorder:
     b[i] = y * z;  // y used earlier
     a[i] = b[i-1] + y;
     // Before reorder:
     a[i] = b[i-1] + y;  // y is invariant
     b[i] = a[i+1] * z;
     
     // After reorder:
     b[i] = a[i+1] * z;
     a[i] = b[i-1] + y;
     ```
   - Cluster memory operations with similar addresses 

Loop Distribution:
**When to Apply**:  
   - Multiple independent write targets in loop body  
   - Example:  
     ```c
     // Original
     for(i) {
       A[i] = ...;  // Writes to A
       B[i] = ...;  // Writes to B (independent)
     }
     
     // Distributed
     for(i) { A[i] = ...; }  
     for(i) { B[i] = ...; }  
     ```  
   *Validation*:  
   - Distributed loops must have identical iteration ranges  
   - No interleaved access between distributed loops

b[i] = b[i+1] - e[i] * d[i];   // writes b[i], affecting later a[i+1] in same nl
```

Key dependency:  
- `a[i]` uses `b[i-1]` computed in the **same nl iteration** at `i-1` (if i>1), except `b[0]` is from previous nl iteration or initial value.  
- `b[i]` uses `b[i+1]` from **previous nl iteration** (if i+1 not yet updated in this nl) or from current nl if i+1 was already processed? Wait — in original loop, i increases, so `b[i+1]` is from previous nl iteration (since i+1 > i, not yet updated in this nl).  

So original:  
`b[i] = old_b[i+1] - e[i]*d[i]`  
`a[i] = old_b[i-1] + c[i]*d[i]` (where `old_b[i-1]` is from previous nl if i=1, else from current nl’s earlier iteration).  

---

**Optimized code behavior:**  
First loop (reverse order):  
`b[i] = b[i+1] - e[i]*d[i]` for i from LEN_1D-2 down to 1.  

In reverse order, `b[i+1]` is **already updated** in this nl if i+1 < LEN_1D-1? Let’s check:  
Start: i = LEN_1D-2: b[i+1] = b[LEN_1D-1] (unchanged in this nl, from previous nl).  
Then i = LEN_1D-3: b[i+1] = b[LEN_1D-2] (already updated in this nl!).  

So in optimized version, b[i] uses **updated b[i+1]** from current nl, unlike original which used b[i+1] from previous nl. This changes results.  

Second loop (forward order):  
`a[i] = b[i-1] + c[i]*d[i]` where b[i-1] is from **updated b[i-1]** from first loop (current nl).  

Thus semantics differ.  

---

**Example test:**

Original (i=1,2):  
nl=1:  
i=1: a1 = b0 + 0 = b0; b1 = b2 - 1 = b2-1  
i=2: a2 = b1(new) + 0 = b2-1; b2 = b3 - 1 = b3-1  

Optimized:  
First loop (reverse):  
i=2: b2 = b3 - 1  
i=1: b1 = b2(new) - 1 = (b3-1)-1 = b3-2  
Second loop:  
i=1: a1 = b0 (unchanged)  
i=2: a2 = b1(new) = b3-2  

Compare final b1: original b1 = b2-1, optimized b1 = b3-2.  
If b2 != b3, results differ.  

Thus semantics are inconsistent — unit test analysis is correct.  

---

**3. Conclusion**  
The optimized code changes the meaning due to loop order reversal without preserving the original dependency on `b[i+1]` from previous iteration of nl.

Loop Splitting:  
**Type 1 - Instruction Splitting**  
   *When to use*:  
   - Multiple independent statements exist within the loop body  
   - Example pattern:  
     ```c
     for(i) { 
       A[i] = ...;  // Independent group 1
       B[i] = ...;  // Independent group 2 
     }
     ```  
   *Implementation*:  
   ```c
   for(i) { A[i] = ...; }  // Split loop 1  
   for(i) { B[i] = ...; }  // Split loop 2  
   ```  
   *Validation*:  
   - Total iteration count remains identical  
   - Memory access order preserved across all arrays  

**Type 2 - Iteration Range Splitting**  
   *When to use*:  
   - Loop contains phase-dependent patterns (e.g., mid-point update)  
   - Vectorization blocked by conditional value changes  
   - Example pattern:  
     ```c
     for(i=0; i<LEN; i++) a[i] = a[mid] + b[i];  
     ```

   ```c

   for(i=0; i<mid; i++) a[i] = a[mid] + b[i];  
   // Mid-point update  
   a[mid] = a[mid] + b[mid];  
   // Phase 2: i=mid+1~end (post-update)  
   for(i=mid+1; i<LEN; i++) a[i] = a[mid] + b[i];  
   ```  
   *Validation Checklist*:  
   - Split boundaries cover original iteration range  
   - No overlapping writes between split segments  

Loop Reordering and Instruction Reordering:
**Application Rules**:

     a[i] = x + y;  // y is invariant
     b[i] = y * z;
     
     // After reorder:
     b[i] = y * z;  // y used earlier
     a[i] = x + y;
     ```

    // Before reorder:
     a[i] = b[i-1] + y;  // y is invariant
     b[i] = y * z;
     
     // After reorder:
     b[i] = y * z;  // y used earlier
     a[i] = b[i-1] + y;
     // Before reorder:

     b[i] = a[i+1] * z;
     
     // After reorder:
     b[i] = a[i+1] * z;
     a[i] = b[i-1] + y;
     ```
   - Cluster memory operations with similar addresses

   - No interleaved access between distributed loops

**Objective**: Generate a semantically equivalent vectorize-friendly version of `*_opt` using:  
**Preservation Requirements**:  
1. Function signature

If the original value is used, e.g. a[i+1] is used for iteration i, a[i+1] should be stored in a temporary variable(use memcpy to store orign value) or used before updating a[i+1]
If the updated value is used, e.g. a[i-1] is used for iteration i, a[i-1] should update before using it.

**Possible Transformations**  
Loop Splitting:  
**Type 1 - Instruction Splitting**  
   *When to use*:  
   - Multiple independent statements exist within the loop body  
   - Example pattern:  
     ```c
     for(i) { 
       A[i] = ...;  // Independent group 1
       B[i] = ...;  // Independent group 2 
     }
     ```  
   *Implementation*:  
   ```c
   for(i) { A[i] = ...; }  // Split loop 1  
   for(i) { B[i] = ...; }  // Split loop 2  
   ```  
   *Validation*:  
   - Total iteration count remains identical  
   - Memory access order preserved across all arrays  

**Type 2 - Iteration Range Splitting**  
   *When to use*:  
   - Loop contains phase-dependent patterns (e.g., mid-point update)  
   - Vectorization blocked by conditional value changes  
   - Example pattern:  
     ```c
     for(i=0; i<LEN; i++) a[i] = a[mid] + b[i];  
     ```  
   *Implementation*:  
   ```c
   // Phase 1: i=0~mid (pre-update)  
   for(i=0; i<mid; i++) a[i] = a[mid] + b[i];  
   // Mid-point update  
   a[mid] = a[mid] + b[mid];  
   // Phase 2: i=mid+1~end (post-update)  
   for(i=mid+1; i<LEN; i++) a[i] = a[mid] + b[i];  
   ```  
   *Validation Checklist*:  
   - Split boundaries cover original iteration range  
   - No overlapping writes between split segments  

Loop Reordering and Instruction Reordering:
**Application Rules**:  
   *Priority Patterns*:  
   - Move loop-invariant computations upward  
     ```c
     // Before reorder:
     a[i] = x + y;  // y is invariant
     b[i] = y * z;
     
     // After reorder:
     b[i] = y * z;  // y used earlier
     a[i] = x + y;
     ```  
   - Solve loop dependency
    ```c
    // Before reorder:
     a[i] = b[i-1] + y;  // y is invariant
     b[i] = y * z;
     
     // After reorder:
     b[i] = y * z;  // y used earlier
     a[i] = b[i-1] + y;
     // Before reorder:
     a[i] = b[i-1] + y;  // y is invariant
     b[i] = a[i+1] * z;
     
     // After reorder:
     b[i] = a[i+1] * z;
     a[i] = b[i-1] + y;
     ```
   - Cluster memory operations with similar addresses 

Loop Distribution:
**When to Apply**:  
   - Multiple independent write targets in loop body  
   - Example:  
     ```c
     // Original
     for(i) {
       A[i] = ...;  // Writes to A
       B[i] = ...;  // Writes to B (independent)
     }
     
     // Distributed
     for(i) { A[i] = ...; }  
     for(i) { B[i] = ...; }  
     ```  
   *Validation*:  
   - Distributed loops must have identical iteration ranges  
   - No interleaved access between distributed loops  

Use temporary variable:
Use temp variable to store result to avoid complex memory access

Remove branching logic (if, else if, switch) from loops to enable automatic vectorization:
Replace multi-way conditionals with arithmetic masking, ensuring uniform execution paths across iterations.
**When to Apply**:  
    - Example:  
     ```c
    // Original
    if (cond1)  
        x = value_A;  
    else if (cond2)  
        x = value_B;  
    else  
        x = value_C;
    // Optimized  
    x = cond1 * value_A + (cond2 && !cond1) * value_B + (!cond1 && !cond2) * value_C;  
     ```

**Preservation Requirements**:  
1. Function signature

If the original value is used, e.g. a[i+1] is used for iteration i, a[i+1] should be stored in a temporary variable(use memcpy to store orign value) or used before updating a[i+1]

**Possible Transformations**  
Loop Splitting:  
**Type 1 - Instruction Splitting**  
   *When to use*:  
   - Multiple independent statements exist within the loop body  
   - Example pattern:  
     ```c
     for(i) { 
       A[i] = ...;  // Independent group 1
       B[i] = ...;  // Independent group 2 
     }
     ```  
   *Implementation*:  
   ```c
   for(i) { A[i] = ...; }  // Split loop 1  
   for(i) { B[i] = ...; }  // Split loop 2  
   ```  
   *Validation*:  
   - Total iteration count remains identical  
   - Memory access order preserved across all arrays  

**Type 2 - Iteration Range Splitting**  
   *When to use*:  
   - Loop contains phase-dependent patterns (e.g., mid-point update)  
   - Vectorization blocked by conditional value changes  
   - Example pattern:  
     ```c
     for(i=0; i<LEN; i++) a[i] = a[mid] + b[i];  
     ```  
   *Implementation*:  
   ```c
   // Phase 1: i=0~mid (pre-update)  
   for(i=0; i<mid; i++) a[i] = a[mid] + b[i];  
   // Mid-point update  
   a[mid] = a[mid] + b[mid];  
   // Phase 2: i=mid+1~end (post-update)  
   for(i=mid+1; i<LEN; i++) a[i] = a[mid] + b[i];  
   ```  
   *Validation Checklist*:  
   - Split boundaries cover original iteration range  
   - No overlapping writes between split segments  

Loop Reordering and Instruction Reordering:
**Application Rules**:  
   *Priority Patterns*:  
   - Move loop-invariant computations upward  
     ```c
     // Before reorder:
     a[i] = x + y;  // y is invariant
     b[i] = y * z;
     
     // After reorder:
     b[i] = y * z;  // y used earlier
     a[i] = x + y;
     ```  
   - Solve loop dependency
    ```c
    // Before reorder:
     a[i] = b[i-1] + y;  // y is invariant
     b[i] = y * z;
     
     // After reorder:
     b[i] = y * z;  // y used earlier
     a[i] = b[i-1] + y;
     // Before reorder:
     a[i] = b[i-1] + y;  // y is invariant
     b[i] = a[i+1] * z;
     
     // After reorder:
     b[i] = a[i+1] * z;
     a[i] = b[i-1] + y;
     ```
   - Cluster memory operations with similar addresses 

Loop Distribution:
**When to Apply**:  
   - Multiple independent write targets in loop body  
   - Example:  
     ```c
     // Original
     for(i) {
       A[i] = ...;  // Writes to A
       B[i] = ...;  // Writes to B (independent)
     }
     
     // Distributed
     for(i) { A[i] = ...; }  
     for(i) { B[i] = ...; }  
     ```  
   *Validation*:  
   - Distributed loops must have identical iteration ranges  
   - No interleaved access between distributed loops

Replace multi-way conditionals with arithmetic masking, ensuring uniform execution paths across iterations.

    // Original
    if (cond1)  
        x = value_A;  
    else if (cond2)  
        x = value_B;  
    else  
        x = value_C;
    // Optimized  
    x = cond1 * value_A + (cond2 && !cond1) * value_B + (!cond1 && !cond2) * value_C;

Looking at the original loop, there's a loop-carried dependency on `b[i]` between the two statements. The `a[i]` calculation uses `b[i-1]` from the previous iteration's update, while `b[i]` uses `b[i+1]` which hasn't been updated yet in the current iteration. To make this vectorization-friendly while preserving semantics, we can use temporary arrays to store the new `b` values before computing `a`.

```c
void s211_opt(int iterations, int LEN_1D, float*a, float*b,float*c,float*d,float*e)
{
    float* b_new = (float*)malloc(LEN_1D * sizeof(float));
    
    for (int nl = 0; nl < iterations; nl++) {
        // First compute all new b values in a temporary array
        for (int i = 1; i < LEN_1D-1; i++) {
            b_new[i] = b[i+1] - e[i] * d[i];
        }
        
        // Then compute a[i] using original b[i-1] values
        for (int i = 1; i < LEN_1D-1; i++) {
            a[i] = b[i-1] + c[i] * d[i];
        }
        
        // Finally copy b_new back to b for next iteration
        for (int i = 1; i < LEN_1D-1; i++) {
            b[i] = b_new[i];
        }
    }
    
    free(b_new);
}

The source code loop is:

```c
for (int i = 1; i < LEN_1D-1; i++) {
    a[i] = b[i - 1] + c[i] * d[i];
    b[i] = b[i + 1] - e[i] * d[i];

In the original, `b[i]` is updated **in-place** in the same iteration, and later iterations use the **updated** `b[i-1]` when computing `a[i]`? Wait — careful:

- `a[i]` uses `b[i-1]`, which was updated in iteration `i-1` (since `b[i-1]` is written in iteration `i-1`).
- `b[i]` uses `b[i+1]`, which is the **original** `b[i+1]` from before this loop iteration (since iteration `i+1` hasn’t run yet in this sweep).

So there’s a **true dependence** from `b[i]` (write) to `a[i+1]` (read of `b[i]` in next iteration).

The optimized code splits into:

1. Compute `b_new[i] = b[i+1] - e[i] * d[i]` for all `i`.
2. Compute `a[i] = b[i-1] + c[i] * d[i]` for all `i`.
3. Copy `b_new` to `b`.

But in step 2, `b[i-1]` is the **original** `b[i-1]` from before the loop (since we haven’t overwritten `b` yet), whereas in the original, `b[i-1]` is the **new** value computed in iteration `i-1` of the same sweep.

Thus the optimized code uses **old** `b[i-1]` for `a[i]`, while the original uses **new** `b[i-1]`. This changes semantics.

Step 1: `b_new[1] = b2 - e1*d1`, `b_new[2] = b3 - e2*d2`  
Step 2: `a[1] = b0 + c1*d1`, `a[2] = b1 + c2*d2` ← here `b1` is old, not `b_new[1]`.  
Step 3: copy `b_new` to `b`.

So `a[2]` differs from original.

---

Thus **semantics are inconsistent**.

Thus the optimized code uses **old** `b[i-1]` for `a[i]`, while the original uses **new** `b[i-1]`. This changes semantics.

---

**Example** (small case):

```

c = [c0, c1, c2, c3]

e = [e0, e1, e2, e3]
```

Original execution (i=1 to 2):

i=1:  
`a[1] = b0 + c1*d1`  
`b[1] = b2 - e1*d1`

i=2:  
`a[2] = b[1] (new) + c2*d2`  
`b[2] = b3 - e2*d2`

So `a[2]` depends on new `b[1]`.

---

Optimized code:

Step 1: `b_new[1] = b2 - e1*d1`, `b_new[2] = b3 - e2*d2`  
Step 2: `a[1] = b0 + c1*d1`, `a[2] = b1 + c2*d2` ← here `b1` is old, not `b_new[1]`.  
Step 3: copy `b_new` to `b`.

So `a[2]` differs from original.

---

Thus **semantics are inconsistent**.

---

### **3. Conclusion**
Since semantics differ, we stop here. The optimized code is incorrect.

**Possible Transformations**  
Loop Splitting:  
**Type 1 - Instruction Splitting**  
   *When to use*:  
   - Multiple independent statements exist within the loop body  
   - Example pattern:  
     ```c
     for(i) { 
       A[i] = ...;  // Independent group 1
       B[i] = ...;  // Independent group 2 
     }
     ```  
   *Implementation*:  
   ```c
   for(i) { A[i] = ...; }  // Split loop 1  
   for(i) { B[i] = ...; }  // Split loop 2  
   ```  
   *Validation*:  
   - Total iteration count remains identical  
   - Memory access order preserved across all arrays  

**Type 2 - Iteration Range Splitting**  
   *When to use*:  
   - Loop contains phase-dependent patterns (e.g., mid-point update)  
   - Vectorization blocked by conditional value changes  
   - Example pattern:  
     ```c
     for(i=0; i<LEN; i++) a[i] = a[mid] + b[i];  
     ```  
   *Implementation*:  
   ```c
   // Phase 1: i=0~mid (pre-update)  
   for(i=0; i<mid; i++) a[i] = a[mid] + b[i];  
   // Mid-point update  
   a[mid] = a[mid] + b[mid];  
   // Phase 2: i=mid+1~end (post-update)  
   for(i=mid+1; i<LEN; i++) a[i] = a[mid] + b[i];  
   ```  
   *Validation Checklist*:  
   - Split boundaries cover original iteration range  
   - No overlapping writes between split segments  

Loop Reordering and Instruction Reordering:
**Application Rules**:  
   *Priority Patterns*:  
   - Move loop-invariant computations upward  
     ```c
     // Before reorder:
     a[i] = x + y;  // y is invariant
     b[i] = y * z;
     
     // After reorder:
     b[i] = y * z;  // y used earlier
     a[i] = x + y;
     ```  
   - Solve loop dependency
    ```c
    // Before reorder:
     a[i] = b[i-1] + y;  // y is invariant
     b[i] = y * z;
     
     // After reorder:
     b[i] = y * z;  // y used earlier
     a[i] = b[i-1] + y;
     // Before reorder:
     a[i] = b[i-1] + y;  // y is invariant
     b[i] = a[i+1] * z;
     
     // After reorder:
     b[i] = a[i+1] * z;
     a[i] = b[i-1] + y;
     ```
   - Cluster memory operations with similar addresses 

Loop Distribution:
**When to Apply**:  
   - Multiple independent write targets in loop body  
   - Example:  
     ```c
     // Original
     for(i) {
       A[i] = ...;  // Writes to A
       B[i] = ...;  // Writes to B (independent)
     }
     
     // Distributed
     for(i) { A[i] = ...; }  
     for(i) { B[i] = ...; }  
     ```  
   *Validation*:  
   - Distributed loops must have identical iteration ranges  
   - No interleaved access between distributed loops  

Use temporary variable:
Use temp variable to store result to avoid complex memory access

    // Optimized

**3. Vectorization analysis**  

Compiler says: loop not vectorized due to backward loop-carried data dependence.  

Let’s see the dependence pattern:  
In optimized code:  
`a[i] = b[i-1] + ...`  
`b[i] = b_new = b[i+1] - ...`  

But `b[i]` is stored, and `b[i-1]` is read in the next iteration. That’s a **true dependence** (RAW) from `b[i]` to `b[i-1]` in iteration `i+1`.  

Specifically:  
Iteration `i+1` reads `b[i]` (as `b[(i+1)-1]`) for computing `a[i+1]`.  
But `b[i]` was written in iteration `i`.  
So distance-1 anti? Wait:  

Actually:  
Read `b[i-1]` at iteration `i` (for `a[i]`).  
Write `b[i]` at iteration `i`.  
Read `b[i]` at iteration `i+1` (as `b[(i+1)-1]`).  

So flow dependence from write `b[i]` at iteration `i` to read `b[i]` at iteration `i+1`. That’s a **forward** dependence, but still loop-carried.  

But vectorization with SIMD processes multiple `i` in parallel; if iteration `i+1` depends on iteration `i`’s `b[i]`, that’s a serial chain.  

However, note: In original code, the dependence is the same:  
`b[i]` updated in iteration `i`, used in iteration `i+1` for `a[i+1]`.  

So original also not vectorizable for same reason.  

But the compiler’s remark on optimized code says “backward loop carried data dependence” — maybe it’s seeing `b[i+1]` read and `b[i]` written, and suspecting a reverse dependence? Let’s check:  

`b_new = b[i+1] - ...` then `b[i] = b_new`.  
So `b[i]` depends on `b[i+1]`. If we think of iteration `i` reading `b[i+1]`, and iteration `i-1` writing `b[i]`, that’s a **backward dependence** (write `b[i]` in iteration `i-1`, read `b[i]` in iteration `i-1`? Wait, no:  

Actually:  
Iteration `i` reads `b[i+1]`.  
Iteration `i+1` writes `b[i+1]`.  
So read in iteration `i` of `b[i+1]` is from iteration `i+1`’s write? That would be a loop-carried dependence from later iteration to earlier — that’s **backward dependence** (anti-dependence).  

But that’s not true here because `i+1` hasn’t happened yet when `i` runs. So `b[i+1]` is from previous `nl` iteration or original. So no loop-carried dependence in the `b[i+1]` access.  

So the only loop-carried true dependence is through `b[i]` to next iteration’s `b[i-1]` read.  

That prevents simple vectorization of the whole loop body.  

**Can we fix?**  
We could split into two separate loops:  
1. Compute all `a[i] = b[i-1] + c[i]*d[i]`  
2. Compute all `b[i] = b[i+1] - e[i]*d[i]`  

But that changes semantics because step 2’s `b[i+1]` in original is original `b[i+1]`, not updated one. In the split version, if we do step 2 after step 1, `b[i+1]` in step 2 is still original (since we haven’t updated `b` yet). So splitting works:  

Loop 1: for i: a[i] = b[i-1] + c[i]*d[i]  
Loop 2: for i: b[i] = b[i+1] - e[i]*d[i]  

Now each loop is vectorizable:  
Loop 1 has dependence distance 1 (b[i-1] is from original b, not updated in this nl), so vectorizable.  
Loop 2 reads b[i+1] from original, writes b[i], no loop-carried dependence.  

Thus, the current optimized code is **not fully optimized** because it still has a loop-carried dependence preventing vectorization, but we can improve via loop distribution.

---

**4. Conclusion**  
The optimized code is semantically correct but not vectorizable due to a true loop-carried dependence (through `b[i]`). Since there is an optimization opportunity (loop distribution) to enable vectorization, the code is not fully optimized.

1. Compute all `a[i] = b[i-1] + c[i]*d[i]`  
2. Compute all `b[i] = b[i+1] - e[i]*d[i]`  

But that changes semantics because step 2’s `b[i+1]` in original is original `b[i+1]`, not updated one. In the split version, if we do step 2 after step 1, `b[i+1]` in step 2 is still original (since we haven’t updated `b` yet). So splitting works:  

Loop 1: for i: a[i] = b[i-1] + c[i]*d[i]  
Loop 2: for i: b[i] = b[i+1] - e[i]*d[i]  

Now each loop is vectorizable:  
Loop 1 has dependence distance 1 (b[i-1] is from original b, not updated in this nl), so vectorizable.  
Loop 2 reads b[i+1] from original, writes b[i], no loop-carried dependence.  

Thus, the current optimized code is **not fully optimized** because it still has a loop-carried dependence preventing vectorization, but we can improve via loop distribution.

**4. Conclusion**  
The optimized code is semantically correct but not vectorizable due to a true loop-carried dependence (through `b[i]`). Since there is an optimization opportunity (loop distribution) to enable vectorization, the code is not fully optimized.

Loop Splitting:  
**Type 1 - Instruction Splitting**  
   *When to use*:  
   - Multiple independent statements exist within the loop body  
   - Example pattern:  
     ```c
     for(i) { 
       A[i] = ...;  // Independent group 1
       B[i] = ...;  // Independent group 2 
     }
     ```  
   *Implementation*:  
   ```c
   for(i) { A[i] = ...; }  // Split loop 1  
   for(i) { B[i] = ...; }  // Split loop 2  
   ```  
   *Validation*:  
   - Total iteration count remains identical  
   - Memory access order preserved across all arrays  

**Type 2 - Iteration Range Splitting**  
   *When to use*:  
   - Loop contains phase-dependent patterns (e.g., mid-point update)  
   - Vectorization blocked by conditional value changes  
   - Example pattern:  
     ```c
     for(i=0; i<LEN; i++) a[i] = a[mid] + b[i];  
     ```  
   *Implementation*:  
   ```c

   for(i=0; i<mid; i++) a[i] = a[mid] + b[i];  
   // Mid-point update  
   a[mid] = a[mid] + b[mid];  
   // Phase 2: i=mid+1~end (post-update)  
   for(i=mid+1; i<LEN; i++) a[i] = a[mid] + b[i];  
   ```  
   *Validation Checklist*:  
   - Split boundaries cover original iteration range  
   - No overlapping writes between split segments  

Loop Reordering and Instruction Reordering:
**Application Rules**:  
   *Priority Patterns*:  
   - Move loop-invariant computations upward  
     ```c
     // Before reorder:
     a[i] = x + y;  // y is invariant
     b[i] = y * z;
     
     // After reorder:
     b[i] = y * z;  // y used earlier
     a[i] = x + y;

     a[i] = b[i-1] + y;  // y is invariant
     b[i] = y * z;
     
     // After reorder:
     b[i] = y * z;  // y used earlier
     a[i] = b[i-1] + y;
     // Before reorder:
     a[i] = b[i-1] + y;  // y is invariant
     b[i] = a[i+1] * z;
     
     // After reorder:
     b[i] = a[i+1] * z;
     a[i] = b[i-1] + y;
     ```
   - Cluster memory operations with similar addresses 

Loop Distribution:
**When to Apply**:  
   - Multiple independent write targets in loop body  
   - Example:  
     ```c
     // Original
     for(i) {
       A[i] = ...;  // Writes to A
       B[i] = ...;  // Writes to B (independent)
     }
     
     // Distributed
     for(i) { A[i] = ...; }  
     for(i) { B[i] = ...; }  
     ```  
   *Validation*:  
   - Distributed loops must have identical iteration ranges  
   - No interleaved access between distributed loops

In iteration `i`, `b[i]` is overwritten. In iteration `i+1`, the first statement uses `b[i]` (which was just updated in iteration `i`).  
So there is a **loop-carried dependency** on `b[i]` from iteration `i` to `i+1`.

```c

}
```
In the first loop, `b[i-1]` is read from the **original** `b` (from before the loop starts for that `nl` iteration).  
In the second loop, `b[i+1]` is read from the **original** `b` (not updated yet in this `nl` iteration).  

But in the original code, the second statement `b[i] = b[i+1] - e[i]*d[i]` reads `b[i+1]` from the **original** `b` of this iteration (not yet updated in this iteration), because `i+1 > i`, so `b[i+1]` hasn’t been overwritten yet in the same iteration.

So there’s a **true dependency** from `b[i]` (write in iteration `i`) to `a[i+1]` (read in iteration `i+1`).

But in the original, `a[2]` uses `b[1]` from **after** the update in iteration `i=1`.  

Thus semantics differ.

**2. Semantic comparison**  

The original loop in the source code is:

```c
for (int i = 1; i < LEN_1D-1; i++) {
    a[i] = b[i - 1] + c[i] * d[i];
    b[i] = b[i + 1] - e[i] * d[i];
}
```

In each iteration, `a[i]` uses the **old** `b[i-1]` (from before this iteration’s update of `b[i-1]`), and `b[i]` uses the **old** `b[i+1]`.  
The updates to `b[i]` happen in increasing `i` order, so `b[i-1]` in the next iteration (`i+1`) will already have been overwritten in the current iteration — but the original code reads `b[i-1]` before it’s overwritten in the same iteration, so there’s no loop-carried dependency on `b[i-1]` for computing `a[i]`.  
However, there is a dependency for `b[i]`: it reads `b[i+1]`, which has not yet been updated in the current iteration (since we go forward in `i`), so `b[i+1]` is still from the previous iteration’s result (or initial values for the first iteration of `nl`).

The optimized code splits into two loops:

```c
for (int i = 1; i < LEN_1D-1; i++) {
    a[i] = b[i-1] + c[i] * d[i];
}
for (int i = LEN_1D-2; i >= 1; i--) {
    b[i] = b[i+1] - e[i] * d[i];
}
```

In the first loop, `b[i-1]` is the value from the **previous iteration of `nl`** (or initial values for `nl=0`), same as in the original loop’s first statement.

But in the original loop, `b[i] = b[i+1] - e[i]*d[i]` uses `b[i+1]` from the **previous iteration of `nl`** (since `i+1 > i` and we haven’t updated `b[i+1]` yet in forward order).  
In the optimized code’s second loop, we go backward (`i` from `LEN_1D-2` down to `1`), so `b[i+1]` in `b[i] = b[i+1] - ...` is the **newly updated** `b[i+1]` from the same `nl` iteration, not the old one. This changes the semantics.

**Example verification**  
Let’s take `LEN_1D = 5`, initial `b = [b0, b1, b2, b3, b4]`, ignore `a`, `c`, `d`, `e` for simplicity (assume `c[i]*d[i] = 0`, `e[i]*d[i] = 1` for all `i`).

Original loop (`i=1..3`):
- i=1: a[1] = b0; b[1] = b2 - 1;
- i=2: a[2] = b1 (old b1); b[2] = b3 - 1;
- i=3: a[3] = b2 (old b2); b[3] = b4 - 1;

So final b: b[1] = b2 - 1, b[2] = b3 - 1, b[3] = b4 - 1.

Optimized code:
First loop: a[1]=b0, a[2]=b1, a[3]=b2.
Second loop (backward):
- i=3: b[3] = b4 - 1
- i=2: b[2] = b[3] - 1 = (b4 - 1) - 1 = b4 - 2
- i=1: b[1] = b[2] - 1 = (b4 - 2) - 1 = b4 - 3

Clearly different from original.

Thus, semantics are **inconsistent**.

---

**3. Conclusion**  
The optimized code changes the meaning of the program due to the backward update of `b[i]` using already-updated `b[i+1]`.  
Since semantics differ, we stop here.

**Objective**: Generate a semantically equivalent vectorize-friendly version of `*_opt` using:  
**Preservation Requirements**:  
1. Function signature

If the original value is used, e.g. a[i+1] is used for iteration i, a[i+1] should be stored in a temporary variable(use memcpy to store orign value) or used before updating a[i+1]
If the updated value is used, e.g. a[i-1] is used for iteration i, a[i-1] should update before using it.

**Possible Transformations**  
Loop Splitting:  
**Type 1 - Instruction Splitting**  
   *When to use*:  
   - Multiple independent statements exist within the loop body  
   - Example pattern:  
     ```c
     for(i) { 
       A[i] = ...;  // Independent group 1
       B[i] = ...;  // Independent group 2 
     }
     ```  
   *Implementation*:  
   ```c
   for(i) { A[i] = ...; }  // Split loop 1  
   for(i) { B[i] = ...; }  // Split loop 2  
   ```  
   *Validation*:  
   - Total iteration count remains identical  
   - Memory access order preserved across all arrays  

**Type 2 - Iteration Range Splitting**  
   *When to use*:  
   - Loop contains phase-dependent patterns (e.g., mid-point update)  
   - Vectorization blocked by conditional value changes  
   - Example pattern:  
     ```c
     for(i=0; i<LEN; i++) a[i] = a[mid] + b[i];  
     ```  
   *Implementation*:  
   ```c
   // Phase 1: i=0~mid (pre-update)  
   for(i=0; i<mid; i++) a[i] = a[mid] + b[i];  
   // Mid-point update  
   a[mid] = a[mid] + b[mid];  
   // Phase 2: i=mid+1~end (post-update)  
   for(i=mid+1; i<LEN; i++) a[i] = a[mid] + b[i];  
   ```  
   *Validation Checklist*:  
   - Split boundaries cover original iteration range  
   - No overlapping writes between split segments  

Loop Reordering and Instruction Reordering:
**Application Rules**:  
   *Priority Patterns*:  
   - Move loop-invariant computations upward  
     ```c
     // Before reorder:
     a[i] = x + y;  // y is invariant
     b[i] = y * z;
     
     // After reorder:
     b[i] = y * z;  // y used earlier
     a[i] = x + y;
     ```  
   - Solve loop dependency
    ```c
    // Before reorder:
     a[i] = b[i-1] + y;  // y is invariant
     b[i] = y * z;
     
     // After reorder:
     b[i] = y * z;  // y used earlier
     a[i] = b[i-1] + y;
     // Before reorder:
     a[i] = b[i-1] + y;  // y is invariant
     b[i] = a[i+1] * z;
     
     // After reorder:
     b[i] = a[i+1] * z;
     a[i] = b[i-1] + y;
     ```
   - Cluster memory operations with similar addresses 

Loop Distribution:
**When to Apply**:  
   - Multiple independent write targets in loop body  
   - Example:  
     ```c
     // Original
     for(i) {
       A[i] = ...;  // Writes to A
       B[i] = ...;  // Writes to B (independent)
     }
     
     // Distributed
     for(i) { A[i] = ...; }  
     for(i) { B[i] = ...; }  
     ```  
   *Validation*:  
   - Distributed loops must have identical iteration ranges  
   - No interleaved access between distributed loops  

Use temporary variable:
Use temp variable to store result to avoid complex memory access

Remove branching logic (if, else if, switch) from loops to enable automatic vectorization:
Replace multi-way conditionals with arithmetic masking, ensuring uniform execution paths across iterations.
**When to Apply**:  
    - Example:  
     ```c
    // Original
    if (cond1)  
        x = value_A;  
    else if (cond2)  
        x = value_B;  
    else  
        x = value_C;
    // Optimized  
    x = cond1 * value_A + (cond2 && !cond1) * value_B + (!cond1 && !cond2) * value_C;  
     ```

Loop Splitting:  
**Type 1 - Instruction Splitting**  
   *When to use*:  
   - Multiple independent statements exist within the loop body  
   - Example pattern:  
     ```c
     for(i) { 
       A[i] = ...;  // Independent group 1
       B[i] = ...;  // Independent group 2 
     }
     ```  
   *Implementation*:  
   ```c
   for(i) { A[i] = ...; }  // Split loop 1  
   for(i) { B[i] = ...; }  // Split loop 2  
   ```  
   *Validation*:  
   - Total iteration count remains identical  
   - Memory access order preserved across all arrays  

**Type 2 - Iteration Range Splitting**  
   *When to use*:  
   - Loop contains phase-dependent patterns (e.g., mid-point update)  
   - Vectorization blocked by conditional value changes  
   - Example pattern:  
     ```c
     for(i=0; i<LEN; i++) a[i] = a[mid] + b[i];  
     ```  
   *Implementation*:  
   ```c
   // Phase 1: i=0~mid (pre-update)  
   for(i=0; i<mid; i++) a[i] = a[mid] + b[i];  
   // Mid-point update  
   a[mid] = a[mid] + b[mid];  
   // Phase 2: i=mid+1~end (post-update)  
   for(i=mid+1; i<LEN; i++) a[i] = a[mid] + b[i];  
   ```  
   *Validation Checklist*:  
   - Split boundaries cover original iteration range  
   - No overlapping writes between split segments

Looking at the original loop, there's a loop-carried dependency: `b[i]` is updated in iteration `i`, and `b[i-1]` is read in the next iteration `i+1` for `a[i+1]`. However, `b[i]` depends on `b[i+1]` (forward dependency), not `b[i-1]`. This means we can reorder the two statements within the loop to compute `b[i]` first (using original `b[i+1]`), then compute `a[i]` (using updated `b[i-1]` from previous iteration).

The key insight is that `b[i]` uses `b[i+1]` which hasn't been modified yet in the current iteration, while `a[i]` uses `b[i-1]` which was updated in the previous iteration. So we should compute `b[i]` before `a[i]` to maintain correctness.

Here's the optimized version:

```c
void s211_opt(int iterations, int LEN_1D, float*a, float*b,float*c,float*d,float*e)
{
    for (int nl = 0; nl < iterations; nl++) {
        // Reorder instructions: compute b[i] first using original b[i+1],
        // then compute a[i] using updated b[i-1]
        for (int i = 1; i < LEN_1D-1; i++) {
            b[i] = b[i+1] - e[i] * d[i];
            a[i] = b[i-1] + c[i] * d[i];
        }
    }
}

Loop Splitting:  
**Type 1 - Instruction Splitting**  
   *When to use*:  
   - Multiple independent statements exist within the loop body  
   - Example pattern:  
     ```c
     for(i) { 
       A[i] = ...;  // Independent group 1
       B[i] = ...;  // Independent group 2 
     }
     ```  
   *Implementation*:  
   ```c
   for(i) { A[i] = ...; }  // Split loop 1  
   for(i) { B[i] = ...; }  // Split loop 2  
   ```  
   *Validation*:  
   - Total iteration count remains identical  
   - Memory access order preserved across all arrays  

**Type 2 - Iteration Range Splitting**  
   *When to use*:  
   - Loop contains phase-dependent patterns (e.g., mid-point update)  
   - Vectorization blocked by conditional value changes  
   - Example pattern:  
     ```c
     for(i=0; i<LEN; i++) a[i] = a[mid] + b[i];  
     ```

   // Phase 1: i=0~mid (pre-update)  
   for(i=0; i<mid; i++) a[i] = a[mid] + b[i];  
   // Mid-point update  
   a[mid] = a[mid] + b[mid];  
   // Phase 2: i=mid+1~end (post-update)  
   for(i=mid+1; i<LEN; i++) a[i] = a[mid] + b[i];  
   ```  
   *Validation Checklist*:  
   - Split boundaries cover original iteration range  
   - No overlapping writes between split segments

**Application Rules**:

     // Before reorder:

     a[i] = x + y;

   - Cluster memory operations with similar addresses 

Loop Distribution:

```c

void s211_opt(int iterations, int LEN_1D, float*a, float*b,float*c,float*d,float*e)
{
    for (int nl = 0; nl < iterations; nl++) {
        // Split the loop into two phases to break the loop-carried dependency
        // Phase 1: Compute all b[i] updates first using original b[i+1] values
        for (int i = 1; i < LEN_1D-1; i++) {
            b[i] = b[i+1] - e[i] * d[i];
        }
        
        // Phase 2: Compute all a[i] using the updated b[i-1] values
        for (int i = 1; i < LEN_1D-1; i++) {
            a[i] = b[i-1] + c[i] * d[i];
        }
    }
}

```
